# KISS Sorcar Feature Inventory

Version 2026.11.7 · HEAD `d522176885` (6,001 commits) · compiled 2026-11-10 · supersedes the 2026-09-30 inventory

Every count and feature in this document was checked against the source tree at `d522176885`, the packaged assets under `src/kiss/`, and the development machine's task database on 2026-11-10. Items marked **NEW** did not exist at the previous inventory's commit `d450c0be9a` (2026-09-30), and items marked **CHANGED** existed there and behave differently now; the 1,075 commits between the two are summarised in section 2. A date such as 10-09 is the author date of the commit that made the change, in 2026. `~/.kiss/` stands for the KISS home directory: `$KISS_HOME` when it is set, otherwise the directory under the user's home that the build's `brand.json` names (`.kiss` in the stock build). Where the code and a document disagree, the code wins and the disagreement is listed in section 24.

## 1. At a glance

- **One agent, many surfaces** **CHANGED**: the same `SorcarAgent` (as its recording subclass `ChatSorcarAgent` wherever a run is stored in `history.db`) runs behind the web app served by the `kiss-web` daemon, the `sorcar` CLI, the Python client API, headless SEA runs, 42 messaging and service channels, and cron jobs. The web app is one page, reached in a browser tab, as an installed PWA, in the `kiss-app` desktop window or from an iOS Home Screen icon; the VS Code extension was removed on 10-14. Every client of a daemon mirrors its tab registry (`~/.kiss/tabs.json`) for chat tabs, and sub-agent and browser tabs are synchronised by events, so an open task, sub-agent or browser tab appears in every client. The `sorcar` CLI and a headless SEA run keep the agent in the calling process, without the daemon.
- **Several computers, one web app** **NEW**: a daemon keeps a registry of other computers' daemons (`~/.kiss/machines.json`) and drives them from its own page: their chats, files, settings, terminals and browser pages appear on the local surfaces, `run_agent(machine=...)` and `Bash(background=True, machine=...)` send work to them, and `/sync` brings every registered machine and the local checkout to the same commit (section 15).
- **Gated finishes** **NEW**: a run that has `Edit` and changed a code file is checked when it calls `finish`, unless its SEA switched the gate off: its own `verify.sh`, when it wrote one, must pass, and for a top-level run a reviewer model reads the diff once (by default the strongest configured model of another family; none when no such model is configured, when `coding_gate_reviewer` is `off`, or when a read-only review the agent dispatched itself already covers the final diff); a finish is refused for at most three rounds, after which the work is delivered with the open findings attached (section 6).
- **1,077 model entries** **CHANGED** in `src/kiss/core/models/MODEL_INFO.json` (1,033 generation, 885 with function calling, 7 embedding, 30 typed decisions; 623 base models and 454 effort aliases): 238 direct API models (OpenAI 89, Anthropic 82, Gemini 49, Moonshot 10, Z.AI 8), 496 through OpenRouter, 144 Claude models through Amazon Bedrock, 109 through Together AI, and 79 `cc/` (Claude Code) and 11 `codex/` CLI-backed entries, driven as plain models with the KISS system prompt and tools and billed to the Claude or ChatGPT subscription by default. Five router entries (`autorouter`, `bestrouter`, `orautorouter`, `orbestrouter`, `ccxbestrouter`) appear in the model picker and are implemented as SEAs (section 8).
- **30 bundled Sorcar Extension Agents** **CHANGED** in `src/kiss/agents/seas/` (31 folders with the shared `base`): 28 slash commands, 2 hidden (`coding`, `sorcar`) and 5 model-picker entries; 13 were added since 2026-09-30. `src/kiss/agents/third_party_agents/` adds 44 SEA folders (43 commands, 42 of them channel SEAs), and `/cron` is built in, so a registry with an empty `SEAS.md` lists 72 commands. Every SEA defines exactly one `BaseSea` subclass, a bundled one in `<name>/<name>_sea.py`, and every registered command defines `description()` (section 9).
- **44 built-in tool functions** **CHANGED** in a full-profile top-level run on the host, in parallel mode with web tools and memory on and the decisions model available (shell 3, files 3, checkpoints 2, worktree 2, browser 15, memory 7, dispatch 4, MCP sign-in 2, interaction 6 including `finish` and, when the decisions model is available, `decide`), before skill, MCP-server and SEA-supplied tools; fifteen tool profile names (`full`, `review`, `assistant`, `bash`, `none` and the ten tool groups `shell`, `edit`, `browser`, `memory`, `agents`, `mcp`, `skills`, `user`, `decide`, `control`), joinable with `+` (section 7).
- **54 console scripts** in `pyproject.toml`: `sorcar`, `kiss-web`, `kiss-cron`, `kiss-app`, `sea`, `check`, `impacted-tests`, `findings-report`, `generate-api-docs`, `swedefend-eval` and 44 `kiss-<channel>` CLIs.
- **Prompt assets**: `SYSTEM.md` 7,083 words, `SYSTEM_LITE.md` 1,074 words, 22 tips and 2 bundled promptlets; `SAMPLE_TASKS.md` (12 sample tasks) still ships, but no code reads it (section 5).
- **Codebase**: 231,293 lines of non-test Python under `src/kiss` (server 45,818; `agents/sorcar` 64,809), 2,073 `test_*.py` files with 14,406 test functions (558,090 lines across the 2,140 Python files under `src/kiss/tests`), the 32,016-line `main.js` of the web app under `src/kiss/webapp/media/` (41,714 lines with the other media scripts and vendored JavaScript) and 407 JS test files (section 22).
- **Task database** (development machine, rows dated before 2026-11-10 UTC): 41,127 task rows (9,685 top-level, 31,442 sub-agent) in 8,614 chats, 23.2 million persisted events, 968,429 tool calls, 1,332 distinct working directories on top-level tasks (1,205 of them per-run cron directories; 2,534 across all rows), 87 named models; 13,165 of the rows ran on 8 other machines and were copied in by the history sync. Recorded spend on top-level tasks is **$77.6K** across 69.8 billion tokens and 675K steps (section 23).

## 2. What changed since 2026-09-30

1,075 commits (931 without the 144 merges) separate the previous inventory at `d450c0be9a` (version 2026.9.27) from `d522176885`: their author dates run from 2026-09-30 to 2026-11-10 (UTC) with a peak of 90 commits on 10-08, and they bump `src/kiss/core/_version.py` 37 times, from 2026.9.28 to 2026.11.7, 31 of those versions carrying a release tag (`v2026.9.28` through `v2026.11.4`).

| Theme | What landed | Commits |
|---|---|---|
| SEA contract and hooks | A SEA is one `BaseSea` subclass per file (`src/kiss/agents/seas/base/base_sea.py`, 10-05); the launcher folds the methods of the class chain, base first, and `BaseSea` is the root layer of every chat-launched run (10-06). Methods: `description`, `settings`, `prompt`, `system_prompt`, `tools`, `llm_call_hook`, `tool_call_hook` and `llm_response_hook` (both return a `Verdict`: `ALLOW`, `refuse`, `end_run`, `allow_with_note`), `register_as_model`, `on_picked_as_model`. `WorkerSea` and `ChannelSea` replace the `kind` and `channel` settings (10-07). 14 settings keys with one precedence rule and `locked` keys; `model_config` flags `plain_io` and `coding_gate`; `@tool_schema` and `LLMReply` (10-31). The `sea` console script (`sea lint`, `sea docs`), headless runs (`headless.py`, `scripts/run_sea.py`) and help for the slash commands a task names. Details in section 9. | `08c60f475d` `7762e96571` `ec0ef731ac` `47f5bacdb5` `16c8eb13a3` `e2ff4cd19f` `a17891dc6d` `5cb7f1f98c` |
| Finish gate | `CodingGate` (`coding_gate.py`, 2,349 lines, 11-01) gates a `SorcarAgent` run with `Edit` that changed a code file or wrote a `verify.sh`: `finish` runs the verifier (900 s cap, a passing run on an unchanged tree is reused); a top-level run also gets one read-only review of the diff (240 s) and a reading check of its `ledger.md` from the model that `coding_gate_reviewer` in `config.json` names (`off` for none), by default the strongest configured model of another family, with neither check when no such model is configured; `finish(success=False)` is checked too, `commit_and_merge` runs the same stages, and at most three rounds are refused. `coding_gate: false` in `model_config` turns the gate off. Details in section 6. | `be211fade3` `3f5736249f` `ea20ae239e` `694f5579e7` `f800eea5a7` |
| Tool-call guards | Shell-rewrite and here-document guards that redirect to `Write` or `Edit`, beside the older read-before-modify rule, which a `Bash` view now satisfies (`edit_guards.py`); a quote-aware shell scanner (`shell_scan.py`); the destructive-command guard and an install timeout of at least 900 s (`shell_guards.py`); a foreground `sleep` over 30 s refused when it outlasts the call's timeout and otherwise spent waiting on the running background job, if there is one and the sleep can be taken out of the command (one that paces a loop cannot); a review sub-agent of its caller's model family moved to another configured family (`second_opinion_model`) unless the call or the SEA's `model` setting pins its model or the daemon pins every task to its model (`KISS_PIN_TASK_MODEL`); and a summary-due note every 10 steps. Details in section 6. | `1509b8cc00` `38a98d0ce1` `efd88240b5` `bf69cb1b67` `79e5c494c1` `f6e4e2bd7f` |

The daemon's Unix socket gave way to a token-authenticated local WSS endpoint (10-01). CHATS merges the registered machines' tasks, and favourite, rename and delete on a remote row go to its machine. `/sync` runs on every registered machine, and `/install` runs `install.sh` on the ssh-registered ones, all or those it names, that hold a clone of the private development repository. Also fail-over that prefers a model of another family and keeps the same-family twin as the last resort (`model_families.py`), a vision bridge that describes images and PDFs to text-only models (`vision_bridge.py`), and Claude Fable 5.2. Fixes correct cache-write billing (OpenAI 12-hour writes, streamed Anthropic writes) and the cache-read rate of Fable 5.2 (0.025x of the input price, 11-10), keep sub-cent costs from the usage record to the UI, bill a call interrupted by Stop, file side calls (classifier, vision bridge, gate review) under the model that made them, and attribute Spend by model through the `model_costs` column. | `7fb08a0a16` `13127873ff` `0c48894feb` `b03e2241e0` `82faa699f8` `e02e6b5357` `d5d65d9d49` `11a1c80124` `d522176885` |

| Cost levers | `routine_turn_effort` (off by default) lowers effort on routine turns; runs that cannot edit files get the short `SYSTEM_LITE` prompt; the task classifier asks the Jev decisions model first by default (10-03) when an OpenRouter key is configured, with the LLM classifier as the fallback, counts any task that could change a repository file as development unless it is git-only (10-02) and returns only `is_simple` since 10-09 (section 11); a reviewer's budget follows the size of the diff (`review_budget`); with prompt caching on, Bedrock Claude requests cache the conversation through a breakpoint that moves to the last cacheable block every turn (10-21, section 8). | `1509b8cc00` `d66f2ea32d` `30b388acb0` `ff7ff8b742` `8609e6ecac` |

| Sub-agents and worktrees | `run_agent` takes its options in the SEA settings vocabulary under one precedence rule (call arguments, the SEA's `settings()`, the caller's settings, user defaults); `wait=false` returns a job that `agent_job` tails, waits for (`wait_any` since 11-09) or kills; `run_parallel` is one `run_agent` job per task; a result carries a `ran:` line that says what ran; the spend of a job that settles after its caller's task row was saved is added to that row and its finished ancestors (10-07). Worktrees are created on demand, one per repository, by `create_worktree(path)`, and `commit_and_merge()` merges them all; a `Write` or `Edit` of a checkout path is refused until its repository has a worktree, and after that it is remapped into the work dir's own worktree or, for a repository isolated elsewhere, refused with the path to use; the SEA settings `use_worktree` and `auto_commit` are gone. | `rsorcar` and release | `rsorcar` no longer copies private ssh keys by default (`SORCAR_SKIP_SSH_COPY`, agent forwarding instead) and deploys while tasks run: it skips only the `kiss-web` restart, and the history sync holds back unfinished tasks and transmits deletions. `sync_db` reconciles `task_history` columns between releases instead of refusing. `scripts/release.sh` publishes one `Release X.Y.Z` commit per release to the public repository (`build_release_chain`), without the paths in `scripts/exclude.json`. | `e55180eb69` `c6595e6bad` `62310fac3b` `30582cb5ce` `09afbb036f` |
| Memory and knowledge | `/sync_history_and_memory` merges `history.db` and `memories/` across this machine and every ssh-registered machine in one run that pulls from every peer and then pushes the union back (`peer_sync.py`, `memory_delta.py`, `ssh_peer.py`). Running tasks are held back for the next run, and a page changed on two machines at times too close to order is reported as a conflict and left as it is on each. The run probes each peer for its state directory (`home_probe`), so installs of different brands sync too. The memory pages most relevant to a task are recalled automatically and listed in the task message. Details in section 13. | `1509b8cc00` `aa06ed068c` `285c8e87c9` `3c2ef2e698` |
| Cron | A repeating job more than 10 minutes overdue is rescheduled without running (`MISSED_RUN_GRACE_SECONDS`), so a daemon restart no longer fires every missed job. Two default jobs are seeded (recommendations and a good-morning greeting). Schedule rows have edit, run-now, enable/disable and delete actions and a `+` button. ## 3. AI discovery and optimisation on real software

**The drivers.** The discovery loop now lives in the `/discover` SEA **NEW** (`src/kiss/agents/seas/discover/discover_sea.py`, added 10-27; section 9) and no longer in `SYSTEM.md`, where it had been a 7-step block. Only a `/discover` run carries it, on `bestrouter` with the full tool profile, and the loop was rewritten **CHANGED** as pseudocode (`DISCOVER`, `ROUND`, `FINISH`). Before the first round it fixes a dev/held-out split, the exact evaluation command and a noise floor in `split.md`. Every evaluated idea is a git commit kept reachable by `refs/discover/<task id>/<id>`; `pareto.md` holds the frontier of non-dominated points and `explored-ideas.md` one row per idea with status `KEPT`, `TIE`, `FAILED`, `DOMINATED` or `OVERFIT` (a `TIE` or `FAILED` idea is never retried). After each round one random draw picks the next move: a variant of a frontier point (below 0.33), a merge of two frontier points or, when no pair qualifies, a fork of an older point (below 0.66), otherwise fresh ideas from an internet search or from one of two other vendors' models. The run ends when a point that meets the goal on dev also passes the held-out check (one that fails is evicted as `OVERFIT`), when the budget is spent, or at the round limit (the task's, else 100). A task that forbids changing its repository is worked in a lab copy and gets `best.patch` back. `SYSTEM.md` keeps the two short blocks on adversarial testing (one sub-task writes breaking tests or workloads, another fixes what they find) and adversarial training, and the held-out and several-candidates rules of "How to Work". The sample prompts "Sorcar for AI Discovery" and "Sorcar for Optimization" now begin with `/discover`; they and the GEPA optimiser prompt, which does not use the SEA, each add a stopping condition ("Do not STOP until ...") and a report. `/rsi7d` and `/revise_and_review_paper` hand their improvement steps to `/discover` (section 9). None of the loop is code: the SEA appends the text to the system prompt and sets the model and tool profile, and the model carries out the rounds with the ordinary tools. A three-round check on a toy classifier (`reports/discover-three-round-check-2026-10-30.html`) found the three files written with the fields the loop asks for and every logged metric reproducible from its commit; its two findings (round commits unreachable after the squash merge, a baseline status that differed between the two files) were fixed the same day. What follows is what the earlier block did when it was pointed at real software, taken from the LaTeX in `papers/`, the logs in `projects/`, the reports in `reports/` and the checked-in copies of the blog under `website/kisssorcar.github.io/blog/`. Every run in the table predates `/discover`. Apart from the collective-communication study, which has nine seeds per problem and an enumerator baseline, each is a single run with no control arm under a task prompt written by the framework's author, so these are records, not experiments.

| Target | Baseline → result | Held-out and correctness gates | Agent effort |
|---|---|---|---|
| **HydraKV, July campaign**: a larger-than-memory key-value store written from scratch (`projects/kv_adversarial/hydra.cc`, 4,467 lines of dependency-free C++17; the README links a public copy, `github.com/ksenxx/hydrakv` release v1.0.0 **NEW**) for YCSB-A: 250 M keys, 100-byte values, Zipf 0.95, 8 GiB memory cap, 64 vCPUs, eight NVMe | Starting engine 1.85 Mops/s → **5.50 Mops/s**, which the main paper puts at ~89% of the 6.2 Mops/s the device allows at the observed 77% cache hit rate. The paper's "5.9× Microsoft FASTER" divides by the 0.93 Mops/s printed in the task text: FASTER was not run in this campaign, and the October rerun in the next row measured it at 4.48 | 250,000,000/250,000,000 keys validated; adversarial workload variants 5.67 (sparse keys), 5.56 (clustered), 3.97 (drifting hot set); held-out 60/25/15 mix generated after the loop closed **3.87** (29% below the scored trace); 28 self-asserting e2e tests, most with a restart leg, clean under ASan/UBSan/TSan, 93% line coverage; three independent audits, each finding bugs the previous round introduced or missed | 8 tasks, 22.6 h, $516, 1,959 steps, 7 continuations, 11 review sub-agents; the human wrote the task prompts and 11 steering messages |
| **HydraKV, from-scratch redo** **NEW** (`papers/kvstorepaper/`, in the MLSys style and aimed at MLSys 2027 by its commits): one run on Claude Fable 5.1 from 10-24 to 10-26 on a public benchmark kit with the July workload and machine class, prompted to write the paper "fresh without consulting any previous experiments and results and memory" | First engine 4.19 Mops/s → **5.99 Mops/s** (median of five) with 1,903 lines of C++ and a write-back cache, on a kit that never scores durability: 1.3× FASTER's 4.48 under the kit's protocol and 1.15× its 5.23 under FASTER's own driver. The user's 10 Mops/s goal was not met; the paper's bounds for this design are 6.8 (device) and 8.3 (CPU) | Variants on the final engine: Zipf 0.90 4.86, drifting 5.68, sparse 6.08, 95% writes 6.96, the kit's validation trace 5.08; after the freeze, YCSB-B 2.25 (FASTER 1.86) and a YCSB-D-derived trace 1.78 (FASTER ahead at 1.92). 43 scored runs passed read-back and the kit's auditor and `validate.sh` read back all 250 M keys; durability is by `Checkpoint` only: killed 30 s into a run, the engine lost 16.2 to 19.5 M acknowledged upserts (19.2 to 23.1%) with no checkpoint and 3.4 to 13.2 M (4.2 to 16.2%) with one every 7 s; six e2e tests run under ASan, UBSan and TSan; the loop found four engine bugs that the kit's integrity checks did not catch, and three recovery bugs got past all of it and were found by a reviewer model reading the source | 16 sessions, about 32.6 h and $511 (the last ten sessions' figures are end-of-session estimates) |
| **Bespoke OLAP TPC-H engine** (VLDB'26 artefact, single-threaded, 22 templates; `projects/bespoke_tpch_x4/`), 32-core Linux, SF10 | 3,132.2 ms → **90.8 ms (34.5×)** summed over 22 queries; 10 queries under 0.1 ms; SF1 276.8 → 14.2 ms (19.5×); ingest 52.5 → 63.0 s, repaid after 4 suite runs; 3.1× ahead of the 284 ms the authors' own multithreaded follow-up reports (a README figure without a run log) | Seed 999 excluded from development: **85.1 ms**, tracking seed 42 query by query; DuckDB as differential oracle on six (SF, seed) configurations; 265-case guard/fallback audit against the pristine artefact; read-only reviewer found an OpenMP race in Q20 and an `int64_t` accumulator in Q1 (baseline uses `__int128`, overflow near SF 8,300), neither visible to the oracle | 3 tasks, 2.4 h, $90, 475 steps; development 1.3 h, 162 steps, $43, zero steering |
| **LZ4** v1.10.0 CLI, multithreaded file mode, 16 cores / 32 threads, 1.7 GB corpus in tmpfs | Level -1 at 32 threads 1,519 → **3,810 MB/s (2.51×)**, 2.57× at 64, 1.88× at 8; with 16 to 64 threads, 1.21–1.27× at level -3 and 7–16% at -6 and -9; scaling 3.6× → 9.1×; faster than pzstd (2,788–2,888) and pigz (1,314) at 32 threads | Compressed output byte-identical to stock at every tested level (-1, -3, -6, -9); 712-case oracle matrix; stock binary decodes patched output; breaker campaign found 2 bugs (a >300 s hang on a 1-byte file, a SIGBUS on mid-run truncation); reviewer found 4 more | ~7.5 h, under $600 |
| **DuckDB** development tree (upstream commit `e500d7786`, 32-core VM), five suites | Geometric means: TPC-H 1.152×, TPC-DS 1.184×, IMDB/JOB 1.215×, h2oai 1.234×, ClickBench 1.237×; largest single query ClickBench q28 ~1.73× (1.76× re-measured after the review fix) | IMDB/JOB, h2oai and ClickBench held out of all PGO training; interleaved A/B because run-to-run drift reached 30%; 6,377-test suite with zero attributable failures; ~15,500 differential hardening statements plus a 43,162-row fuzz corpus and a 6,000-statement randomised corpus byte-identical to stock; reviewer found 5 issues including a NULL-handling regression | not stated |
| **SQLite** 3.54.0 trunk (32-core GCP VM), four benchmarks | speedtest1 2.06×, TATP 1.90×, SSB 1.30×, kvtest 1.25×; **geomean 1.59×** (1.54× at `synchronous=FULL`) | `testrunner.tcl full`: 0 errors in 1,032,940 tests; checksum-verified benchmark harness; 37-script differential corpus under ASan/UBSan; breaker found 2 bugs, a second hardening pass 1; an independent rebuild reproduced **1.50–1.55×** and documented the 2.5% shortfall | under 8 h, under $150 |
| **Biomni × TusoAI biology benchmarks** (reconstructed: three perturbation-response tasks, one enhancer–gene task) | Naive baseline 78.71–92.20 → a single 157-line method scoring **99.66–99.71** on every sealed test set; no reference method (kNN, random forest, gradient boosting on raw features) clears 99 on all four | Sealed train/val/test splits, a regenerated dataset (99.70–99.72), shuffled-label collapse (R² → 0, AUC → 50), process isolation with labels only in the parent; 27 adversarial and security tests; two gate bugs found (a `SystemExit(0)` that passed scoring, `PYTHONHASHSEED`-dependent data) | one autonomous session |
| **Collective communication above AWS Trainium's closed library** (SorcarCCL, a search pipeline inspired by Sorcar, vs OverlayCCL's strategy enumeration), 224 NeuronCores on 7 `trn1.32xlarge` instances | 40 production all-reduce / all-gather / reduce-scatter problems in 8 families: **40/40 confirmed on hardware**; excluding one 117× outlier that the paper puts down to an OverlayCCL compile-time pathology, the speedups run **1.12–4.37×** (median 1.46×, geomean 1.86×), enumerator wins 0; e.g. an MoE routing renormaliser collapsed from 8 all-reduces to 2 (4.06×), a per-block normalisation loop vectorised to `view(B,S)` + `mean(dim=1)` (4.31×); the earlier 143-problem build: 55 anchors, 88 ties, 45 confirmed at 1.06–3.38×, and step time **2.46×** (9.75B Llama-style) and **2.15×** (9.70B GPT-3-class) faster than the enumerator's schedule, which reproduces a textbook baseline the paper calls weaker than the AWS production recipe; both come from 12-step runs at one seed of a training step the authors instrumented with nine collective sites, four of them redundant patterns inserted to exercise a rewrite family, with the rewrites written in by hand and a ZeRO-1 optimiser on a one-seventh shard in place of replicated Adam | Rank-asymmetric correctness oracle at world sizes 4 and 8 plus bf16; nine simulator seeds with Mann-Whitney and bootstrap bounds, then one warm-cache hardware pair per problem; no regression on OverlayCCL's own 8 problems | Capped at 30 steps and $5 of Claude Sonnet 4.5 tokens per problem; on a 24-problem draw the agent cost an estimated $5.37 against the enumerator's $7.77 |

**How the runs went.** The loop's value shows in the ideas it measured and threw away as much as in the ones it kept. In the July campaign HydraKV's first engine (a flat 4-byte-per-key array over 128-byte slots) worked only because the published trace's keys fit in 32 bits, which the main paper calls reward hacking; the first adversarial variant (sparse SplitMix64 keys) OOM-killed it in minutes and forced a fingerprint hash index that scored 1.87 Mops/s. The agent's diagnostics showed a 72% cache hit rate and one contended I/O queue; two rebuilds regressed to 1.10 and 0.47 before an `fio` measurement of the device (718k random 4 KiB reads/s) led to eight raw `io_uring` rings and an asynchronous read-miss state machine (5.27) and SLRU segmentation (5.50). Four further cache designs measured 0.78, 0.95, 1.98 and 4.17 Mops/s and were reverted, and no engine change recovered the drifting-hot-set variant (3.97) without hurting the stationary workloads. The 10 Mops/s goal set mid-run and the 7.0 stretch goal of the later recovery round were never met: at 50% reads the goal needs an 86% hit rate under the device ceiling, and every admission policy tried saturated near 77%. Off the scored mix the engine is far slower (YCSB-B 2.93, YCSB-C 2.78, write-only 5.12, read-modify-write 0.30, delete-heavy time series 0.08).

The October redo **NEW** is recorded iteration by iteration: baseline 4.19, read-allocate 3.06 (27% worse, reverted), a futex stall removed 4.51, one shared CLOCK hand 5.17, submitting each miss at once 4.97 (discarded), relaxed atomic copies for ThreadSanitizer 5.11, a write-back cache 5.99, and in a later session a 48-bit tag index 5.99 (no effect). A second agent instance repeating the first iteration reached 4.38 for $6. The loop stopped after the write-back cache because the session's budget was spent and its own bound said the two remaining ideas could not reach the goal. Its largest finding concerns the baseline, not the engine: the kit's FASTER adapter keeps an audit session open, which stalls FASTER's epoch protection, so FASTER reaches at best 1.23 Mops/s through the adapter as published and 4.48 with a 39-line session patch, 4.8× the kit's printed 0.93. The paper asks that the kit's reference number and the ratios built on it be rerun, and the July "5.9×" divides by that same 0.93. Between the two campaigns sits an archived draft (`papers/kvstorepaper-prev-2026-10-24/`) with a third engine at 7.50 Mops/s, 1.3× the strongest FASTER configuration it measured (5.75). It ran two control seeds without the variant instructions; they scored 5.84 and 7.92 against 7.50 and 5.62 for the protocol seeds, and the draft concluded that "the variants bought no throughput and caught no shortcut". The write-and-review task behind that draft (10-23, 39 h 54 min, $741.62, 65 tasks in the tree) was stopped at round 7 with the reviewer at "weak reject" (`reports/hydra_kv-trajectory-analysis.html`).

On the TPC-H engine the human supplied a researched plan (5,979 characters: remove the `AffinityGuard`, OpenMP first, precomputed join artefacts second), so the agent ran the implement-and-measure step without the search step; its own contributions were the per-query choice of artefact (date-indexed prefix cubes for Q1, Q4–Q8, Q12, Q14, Q15; CSR join indexes for Q9, Q17, Q20; per-order aggregates for Q10, Q18; pre-filtered rows for Q13, Q16, Q19, Q21, Q22), a guarded fallback to the baseline algorithm on every fast path, the profile-driven removal of a serial 60-million-row scan in Q19, and the 265-case audit. Q2 was left alone because its parameters admit no small prefix structure and is the one query slower than baseline (by 0.3 ms); Q10 is 43% of the final total.

The blog projects record rejected ideas the same way. LZ4's `--no-frame-crc` reached 6.4–8.1 GB/s and was rejected as cheating because it changes the output bytes (sequential XXH32 caps the honest path near 3.7 GB/s); DuckDB's row-based aggregate `Combine` rewrite won individual queries (TPC-DS q32 1.088×, q18 1.142×) but moved suite geomeans only 1.006–1.011×, under the pre-registered 1.05× keep bar, and was kept as a patch file; gcc `-march=native` alone gave 0.91–1.16× with regressions on TPC-DS and IMDB, and gcc with PGO and LTO lost to the clang equivalent on every suite. Two of the LZ4 gains were build fixes, not algorithm changes: PGO had silently never applied (the object cache was keyed on the compiler flags, leaving an LTO-only "PGO" binary), and training on level 1 alone cost the HC levels 5–6% until retraining on levels 1, 3 and 9 turned that into +7%.

**What the developer model's own tests missed.** In every project the defects that survived a green test suite were found by a different mechanism: a read-only reviewer from a second vendor (`gpt-5.6-sol`), a breaker sub-agent writing adversarial inputs, or an external audit with its own oracle. The July HydraKV's second audit reproduced four bugs on hardware: an upsert/delete deadlock, an OOM on a full disk, a recovery that dropped 480K of 976K keys and reported success, and deletes 160× slower than upserts (244 µs vs 1.5 µs). The third audit showed that the slow-delete fix had made deletes non-durable: 5,000 of 5,000 deleted keys came back after a kill in the minimal repro. The repair was a delete-intent log written before acknowledgement (final `Delete` 1.0 µs); the reviewer's second round on that repair found a linearisability race between the registry mark and the cache sweep that no fault had triggered. The multi-workload task found that values over 101 bytes had no disk path at all (100% stale reads at 1 KB values) and that clean shutdown lost data on every workload, a bug that predated the task. The October redo **NEW** repeats the pattern at smaller scale. The loop found four bugs that the kit's integrity checks did not catch: a key lost when two writers claimed one free index entry (an aborted scored run), a GC relink that dropped a newer version (a stress test), an evictor acting without its stripe lock (reading the code) and 16 threads parked on one futex (`perf`). Three recovery bugs (an empty value read as padding, a segment unlinked early, a dropped checkpointed tombstone) were found only when a reviewer model read the source, two in round 15 of the paper's review and the third in round 16. A concurrency audit of the reference store of Jitskit, the closest related system **NEW** (`projects/jitskit_refkv_audit/`, 10-22), lists ten issues, all reproduced, among them a checkpoint that stops the world (hot-key read latency 0.026 ms → 1,084 ms), a memory budget that counts value bytes only (RSS 14× the budget) and throughput that falls from 1.58 Mops/s at 4 threads to 0.82 at 16. On the TPC-H engine the DuckDB oracle passed while Q20 had a data race (both racing stores wrote 1) and Q1 accumulated in a narrowed type. LZ4's reviewer found silent corruption when stdin arrived at a non-zero offset; DuckDB's found a NULL-handling change from a hoisted rewrite validation; SQLite's found the engine changes clean and the benchmark harness at fault (failures passed silently through shell pipelines, checksums were printed but not enforced, one fixture was nondeterministic). The blog projects' breaker campaigns found two real bugs each in LZ4, SQLite and Tuso and none in DuckDB. The KISS Sorcar paper's summary of the list ("a delete-durability regression, an aliasing fingerprint, an OpenMP race, a narrowed accumulator") is also its argument for review by a model from another vendor.

**Where the agent went wrong.** Two failure modes are recorded and both changed the prompt. In the July HydraKV's bug-hunting task the fixes were layered onto a rejected slower branch and the engine sat at 3.6 Mops/s until the human asked "did you start with most performant variant of the engine?", which prompted a diff against archived revisions. In the same project the agent once retried an idea its own log had marked failed, which is why the loop, then and now, says such an idea is never retried. The agent's phrase "production-hardened" is contradicted by its own record (three audits, each finding new bugs), and the testing rule's 100% branch coverage was not reached (93.3% of lines and 95.4% of branches at the README's last measurement). The Q1 accumulator was a violation of a stated hard constraint. On the biology benchmark the feature engineering was informed by the documented data generator, which the post says outright. The sqlite-optimized verification found that "full test suite green" held only for the source changes under default build options: the changed runtime defaults fail 26 upstream tests that assert the old defaults, the run with WAL by default was aborted mid-suite and a WAL-default-only build reproduced the `attach2` (26 of 71) and `autovacuum` (23 of 339) failures, and test builds disable computed-goto dispatch, so the suite exercises the fallback path. In the October redo the kit's validation trace was no longer out of sample once the agent had seen its scores, which the paper states.

**The agent as harness.** The same coding rules, cut to 727 words, were run on the 30 Terminal-Bench 2.0 tasks and seven models of the HarnessTax study: 630 attempts, **75.6%** of attempts solved against Pi 70.0%, Codex CLI 65.7% and Claude Code 65.1%, the best point estimate on all seven models (74.1% if solves past 100 turns are counted as failures), inside a pooled interval of 63.8 to 85.9 that covers the other three means. A paired rerun of Pi 0.87.1 on Claude Fable 5 gave +11.1 points (95% interval +3.3 to +20.0) at five cents more per attempt. The same frozen prompt scored 79.1% on the 59 tasks the study did not sample; the main paper's paired Pi rerun on 57 of them gives +10.5 points (+2.9 to +18.7) at 63 cents more per attempt, and the ICML 2027 paper **NEW**, which counts every failed attempt on all 59 as a failure, gives +6.2 (−3.4 to +15.3) at 61 cents more. Discovery, adversarial testing, memory and the reviewer were all switched off for these runs, so they measure the loop, six tools and the rules, not the procedures above.

Terminal-Bench 4.0 **NEW** (the paper's and the reports' name for these runs; 66 tasks sized for hours, five attempts each, 330 per run) runs through a Harbor bridge added on 10-25 (`src/kiss/benchmarks/terminal_bench/`; its launcher names the dataset `terminal-bench/terminal-bench` without a version, section 22), which drives the coding SEA with the browser, web search and memory off. The ICML paper reports three November runs: `claude-opus-5-5` at maximum effort **64.2%** (212 of 330; $10,813; median attempt $28.90 and 131 minutes), 0.6 points under the 64.8% the leaderboard lists for Claude Code on the same model and effort at about $4,700; `gpt-5.6-sol` at xhigh 32.1% (106; $4,075) against Codex's 37.3%; `deepseek-v4.1-flash` at maximum effort 30.3% (100; $930). The October runs kept under `reports/` scored 49.7% (Opus at default effort, $977), 59.1% (Opus at maximum effort, $5,492) and 33.9% (`gpt-5.6-sol`). Most failures are success claims the gate accepted: the hidden tests passed for 164 of 250 accepted claims on Opus, 104 of 313 on GPT and 97 of 287 on DeepSeek, and 84 of the Opus run's 86 wrong claims had the gate's second verifier green as well. A reviewer from another vendor did not rescue the GPT run: in the 29 tasks whose failures were read, the Opus reviewer named the actual defect in 2 and pushed about ten attempts into the change that then failed. The paper's audit of every passing trajectory found no access to the hidden tests or reference solutions and no web search for the benchmark, one reward hack (DeepSeek rebuilt a hidden reference model by brute-forcing its seed; 30.0% without it), two GPT passes that did not meet the task on its merits (31.5% without them) and eight borderline Opus passes (61.8% if all are dropped). The integrity report on the Opus and DeepSeek runs (`reports/terminal-bench-4-integrity-2026-11-07.html`) also names two holes in the harness: the second verifier's script sat world-readable in the agent's container while gate refusals quoted its expected values (Opus opened the script in 200 of 330 attempts), and the agent's log directory was mounted into the container.

**Research artefacts built the same way.** Two July papers sit beside these runs. SWEDefend, written under a prompt ending "Do AI discovery to get better results", is a defence against SWExploit-style backdoored program-repair patches whose combined pipeline at confidence threshold 0.9 catches 97.96% of 49 malicious cases with 0 of 100 benign vetoes where a naive fail-closed judge sat at ~20% false positives, and which reports its own defeat by an adaptive attacker (3 of 3 seeds by the third iteration). Cleverest+ is a fixed-budget (4,3,3) three-member portfolio with sanitiser-signature oracles that solved 6 of 6 issues on a three-subject mini-benchmark in 10 trials per issue (60 in all), with no run yet on the 72-commit benchmark it targets. Two papers are new. The SEA semantics paper **NEW** (`papers/sea/`, a PLDI 2027 paper by its commits) writes the layering, locking and dispatch rules of extension agents (section 9) as an 817-line Python reference with fifteen Lean theorems, and tests the shipped code against it: 47,920 and 95,549 generated checks at two seeds with no disagreement, and 400 live runs through the real runner that all agree. Writing the rules down exposed four corner cases no shipped agent had reached, since repaired, and six of the fifteen disagreements recorded along the way were errors in the reference itself. In its prompt-injection experiment 91 of the 160 runs without the verdict hook had a forbidden effect; with the hook all 302 forbidden calls were refused. The ICML 2027 paper **NEW** (`papers/kisssorcar_icml2027/`) carries the Terminal-Bench 4.0 runs above and the review study below. The redo HydraKV paper went through 17 write-and-review rounds (`reports/hydra_kv-revise-review-log.md`), and its last review recommends "weak accept". `papers/` also gained a one-hour tutorial deck on the harness and SEAs **NEW** (`kisssorcar/tutorial/`, with a `/discover` part since 11-09), a one-page research proposal that applies AI discovery to modelling the Neuron compiler **NEW** (`kisssorcar/agenda/`) and a plain-language explainer of an external paper (`explainers/`).

**The loop turned on the agent itself.** A speed audit of one week of the task database (2,786 tasks) measured where 110 hours of user waiting went: 38% in the agent's own LLM round trips (on `claude-fable-5` a trivial step took 4.4 s at under 25k tokens of context and 9.8 s above 300k) and 33% waiting on `run_parallel`. Reviews, counted inside the `run_parallel` and `run_agent` waits, took 25% of the total, and in the parent tasks with three or more review rounds 0 of 135 rounds came back clean (the same "run all tests" prompt took 0.2 h with 27 parallel splits and 7.9 h with a 12-round review). The cost-lever work that followed removed the mandatory first read of `SORCAR.md` (renamed `AGENTS.md` on 10-03) and the shell-wrapper sub-agents (306 and 258 in the 24-hour baseline before the change, none in the filtered sample of 52 tasks after it; that sample held no reviewer tree, so the baseline's 7 trees whose reviewers took over half the spend have no after figure), reached a 0.970 prompt-cache hit ratio, and then found that its own context compaction was cache-hostile (57 of 57 compactions missed the cache, net +$130), which produced the cache-aware gate and the keep-alive ping (replay: input bill 0.66× of production). Six months of the agent developing its own repository (section 23) gave the controlled result behind the rules: over six task grids the full prompt versus a lite one changed how the agent works (a test file written or changed in 90 of 114 cells against 28) but not the hidden-test pass rate (+0.9 points on the held-out grid, interval −2.8 to +4.1), while two of the four review arms, which the paper calls exploratory, gained on that grid, a second-vendor reviewer 4.7 points (+1.2 to +9.4) at 2.5× the cost and a stronger same-vendor reviewer 3.3 (+0.8 to +6.0) at 3.4×; in the ten-week two-vendor deployment the reviewer made 3 writes in 2,164 calls, all to `tmp/`, took 9.3% of task cost and reported roughly 1,500–2,000 findings, 53% of the coded sample functional bugs.

The ICML 2027 paper **NEW** repeats the review experiment from matched checkpoints, 12 tasks and 36 cells per arm. Review by a second vendor's model with a bounded repair gained 9.2 points of hidden-test pass rate over the checkpoint and 4.2 over an extra pass by the developer model (+1.6 to +6.9, although the task-level sign test does not reject, p = 0.227). A same-model reviewer gained no more than the extra pass (+4.7 against +5.0), and the stronger same-vendor model simply continuing the work came within 0.3 points of second-vendor review (−3.8 to +3.2). Switching the tool guards off changed nothing measurable: they fired in 1 of 24 cells and the pass rate moved by −1.1 points (−4.3 to +1.7).

Terminal-Bench 4.0 was then used to measure the harness's own mechanisms **NEW**. An A/B of the finish gate on 24 of its tasks (11-04 to 11-05, `reports/gate-redesign-ab-2026-11-04/`) set the old gate (a same-model reviewer, up to three reviews, a second agent writing a blind verifier) against the current one (one review by a model of another family with 240 s to answer; section 6): 31 of 46 finished attempts passed under the old gate (67.4%) and 41 of 64 under the new (64.1%), the cost per attempt fell from $22.54 to $12.78, and the time the gate held the agent fell from 27.3 to 2.6 minutes on average. A second A/B of seven harness changes on the same 24 tasks (11-07, `reports/harness-improvements-ab-2026-11-07/`) ran the changed harness twice against a before arm of 28 passes in 46 attempts at a mean $15.08 per attempt. As proposed it passed 33 of 47 at $29.24; with the context-edit settings retuned it passed 33 of 48 at $12.95 (paired over the 23 tasks both arms completed, a cost ratio of 0.84 with an interval of 0.65 to 1.06) in 58.1 agent minutes against 80.2, a figure the report calls confounded because that run had the host to itself; the report says 48 attempts per arm cannot show a pass-rate change of a few points and claims none. The analysis behind those seven changes (`reports/kiss-sorcar-harness-improvements-2026-11-07.html`) read all eight runs to date (2,505 trials, 493,657 model turns, $30,900). It found that 61–66% of the spend of the four maximum-effort runs fell on steps with more than 150k tokens of context, that DeepSeek copied a context-compaction stub back into its own tool calls 1,152 times in 257 of 330 trials, and that the Opus and DeepSeek runs held 944 and 1,579 foreground sleeps of more than 30 s.

**Provenance.** The headline figures above are `\newcommand` macros, in most papers recomputed by a script beside the LaTeX (`kisssorcar/ks_numbers.py` and `ks_tb2.py`, `sesorcar/se_numbers.py`, `sea/make_numbers.py`, `kisssorcar_icml2027/icml_numbers.py` and `tb4_november.py`, the Collective Algebra `compute_numbers.py`) from checked-in evidence (`kisssorcar/evidence/`, the 5,132-file `kisssorcar/ablation/`, `projects/kv_adversarial/DISCOVERY_LOG.md`, `projects/bespoke_tpch_x4/results/`, the 858-file `sorcarccl-production-set-main/`, the redo's 776-file `kvstorepaper/artifact/`). By `grep -c '\\newcommand'` the main paper defines 325 macros, `sesorcar/macros.tex` 2,495, `sea/numbers.tex` 2,239, `kisssorcar_icml2027/macros.tex` 799 and the redo's `hydra_kv.tex` 656, the last with a comment naming each macro's log and no script. `git ls-files papers` counts 9,084 files in 14 directories. Where sources disagree the numbers above follow the latest paper: the HydraKV process record is 8 tasks / 22.6 h / $516 in `kiss_sorcar.tex` but 6 / 17.2 h / $352 in `ks_assistant.tex` (which also attributes the starting engine to Claude Code, not to a Sorcar task); the held-out drop is "29%", "more than a quarter" and "a third" in three places; `kiss_sorcar.tex` still describes the discovery loop as a `SYSTEM.md` block and still points to `papers/kvstorepaper/` for the July standalone paper, which the redo replaced on 10-25; the archived Hacker News draft (`papers/kvstorepaper-prev-2026-10-24/social/hackernews.md`) still says six prompts, ~3,970 lines and 5.51 Mops/s; the two papers that report the Terminal-Bench 2.0 rerun on the unsampled tasks headline different accountings of it (+10.5 on 57 tasks, +6.2 on 59), and the second-vendor review interval starts at +1.2 in one and +1.1 in the other; the redo paper says its three recovery bugs were found one per review round where its review log (`reports/hydra_kv-revise-review-log.md`), followed above, has two in round 15 and one in round 16; the Collective Algebra paper exists twice with 55 vs 40 anchors and the end-to-end step-time result only in the older build; the Cleverest+ comparison table still calls its portfolio allocation "proposed, not yet wired" in one row that its results section contradicts. The `/write_paper` SEA's `check_paper` gate (AI-slop and consistency checks) and `build_paper` (pdflatex + bibtex) run on every revision (section 9; 87 of the 591 slash-command tasks in the database are paper tasks).

**Website** (`website/kisssorcar.github.io/`, 49 tracked files; figures from the checked-in copy, the live site was not read): the home page (`website/kisssorcar.github.io/index.html`, 3,917 lines, with the Terminal-Bench 2.0 table and the paired-Pi numbers) and its Markdown twin, 12 docs pages (`docs/benchmarks.md` **NEW**, the Terminal-Bench 2.0 comparison), 8 blog pages (the six posts used above and two **NEW** pages on the 10-14 removal of the VS Code extension: a 23.5 MB shared-chat export and its trajectory analysis of 48 agents over 4 h 45 min), six paper PDFs under `assets/`, `privacy.html`, `llms.txt` / `llms-full.txt` and a 25-URL `sitemap.xml`. The copy lags the repository. `llms.txt` and the home page list the same six PDFs (`kiss_sorcar`, `se_kiss_sorcar`, `writing_paper`, `hydra_kv`, `swedefend`, `cleverest_plus`) and none of `fable_sol`, `ks_assistant`, `sesorcar`, Collective Algebra, the SEA paper or the ICML paper; `website/kisssorcar.github.io/assets/hydra_kv.pdf` is still the July paper; the two VS Code pages are in the sitemap but in neither blog list; and no page mentions Terminal-Bench 4.0.

## 4. Architecture

```
 ┌────────────────────────────────┐ ┌─────────────────────────────────┐ ┌───────────────────────────┐
 │ Web app page                   │ │ Same-machine API clients        │ │ No daemon: the agent runs │
 │ browser tab · installed PWA    │ │ sorcar.run() Python API         │ │ in the caller's process   │
 │ kiss-app window · iOS Web Clip │ │ cron scheduler · run_agent tool │ │ sorcar CLI                │
 │ auth: password; none for a     │ │ 44 kiss-<channel> CLIs          │ │ headless.run_sea()        │
 │ page opened at a loopback URL  │ │ auth: token, sorcar-local.json  │ │ scripts/run_sea.py        │
 └────────┬───────────────────────┘ └────────┬────────────────────────┘ └────────────┬──────────────┘
          │ WebSocket /ws on one port (8787) │ plain on loopback, TLS elsewhere      │ run_sea runs a
          │ 88 JSON commands (sorcar.py API) │ one object per frame, events by tab   │ SorcarAgent;
 ┌────────▼──────────────────────────────────▼────────────────────────┐              │ run_agent and
 │ kiss-web daemon  src/kiss/server (37 modules, 45,818 lines)        │              │ run_parallel
 │ RemoteAccessServer: HTTP + WebSocket listener, auth, tunnel, TLS   │              │ need a daemon
 │ ServerApi -> AgentServer: TabRegistry (~/.kiss/tabs.json), replay, │              │
 │   sub-agent tab lifecycle, task_runner, merge_flow, autocomplete   │              │
 │ WebPrinter (json_printer): events to a task's tabs and history.db  │              │
 │ tabs: browser_tab + frame_flow · terminal_tab · explorer           │              │
 │ panels: sidebar_panels (Schedule, Machines, Apps, Spend,           │              │
 │   Recommendations) · task_update · file_index · speech             │              │
 │ machines: remote_tabs · remote_views · machine_stream forward a    │              │
 │   tab's commands to the kiss-web daemon of a registered machine    │              │
 │ background: cron scheduler thread · SEA registry watcher (2 s) ·   │              │
 │   services.d helpers · stall_watchdog · deferred_restart           │              │
 └────────┬───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘              │
          │ one agent per task or sub-agent                                          │
 ┌────────▼──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────▼──────────────┐
 │ ChatSorcarAgent ⊂ SorcarAgent ⊂ RelentlessAgent, which runs one KISSAgent per session            │
 │ tools (44 + finish): shell 4 · edit 6 (create_worktree, commit_and_merge, checkpoint,            │
 │   rollback) · browser 15 · memory 7 · agents 4 (run_agent, agent_job, run_parallel,              │
 │   number_of_cores) · mcp 2 · skill · ask_user_question, talk · decide · summary,                 │
 │   set_model; plus the tools of connected MCP servers and of the run's SEA                        │
 │ prompt: SYSTEM.md (SYSTEM_LITE.md for a simple task or a run that cannot edit) + SEA             │
 │   system_prompt() + memory protocol + Task Settings + ~/.kiss/AGENTS.md                          │
 │ coding gate on finish · usage ledger · context compaction · prompt-cache keep-alive ·            │
 │   fallback model · tools on the host or in a Docker container (docker_image)                     │
 └────────┬─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
          │
 ┌────────▼─────────────────────┐ ┌──────────────────────────────┐ ┌────────────────────────────────┐
 │ Models (1,077 entries)       │ │ State in ~/.kiss             │ │ Extension points               │
 │ Anthropic · OpenAI · Gemini  │ │ history.db · config.json     │ │ SEAs (seas/, SEAS.md folders,  │
 │ Amazon Bedrock · OpenRouter  │ │ api_keys.env · tabs.json     │ │ third_party_agents/): tools()  │
 │ Together · Z.ai · Moonshot   │ │ memories/ · cron/jobs.json   │ │ and hooks · skills (SKILL.md)  │
 │ cc/ and codex/ CLIs          │ │ machines.json · tls/         │ │ MCP servers (mcp.json)         │
 │ MY_MODELS.json overrides     │ │ AGENTS.md · AUTOROUTER.md    │ │ promptlets (MY_INJECTION.md)   │
 │ 5 router SEAs in the picker  │ │ sorcar-local.json            │ │ white-label brand (brand.json) │
 └──────────────────────────────┘ └──────────────────────────────┘ └────────────────────────────────┘
```

Figure 1. The data path from clients to models. Every client of the daemon sends commands of one catalogue (`src/kiss/server/sorcar.py`, `API`, 88 commands) over the same WebSocket listener and receives the same types of JSON event. Delivery of a task's events is scoped by tab: one copy per subscribed tab, stamped with its `tabId`, goes to every page (which filters by tab) and to the same-machine clients that addressed that tab or none; the copies of a tab closed on every page go to those clients alone, and another daemon's machine link receives replies and global events but no task events. A web app page signs in with the remote password, which only a page opened at a loopback address (`http://127.0.0.1:8787` or `localhost`) and reaching the daemon directly does not need (section 14); a same-machine program presents the per-start token of `~/.kiss/sorcar-local.json`. The daemon runs the agent for every client except the `sorcar` CLI and the headless `run_sea`, which run it in their own process, the CLI as a `ChatSorcarAgent` and `run_sea` as a plain `SorcarAgent`; in both, `run_agent` and `run_parallel` still submit to a daemon and fail without one. A `kiss-<channel>` CLI starts a private in-process daemon on an ephemeral loopback port and submits to it through `sorcar.run`.

What the figure compresses:

- **One transport.** The Unix socket `~/.kiss/sorcar.sock` was removed 10-01. Local clients use the listener browsers use, and since 10-15 that listener speaks plain HTTP to loopback peers and TLS to everyone else (section 14).
- **One client codebase.** The VS Code extension was removed 10-14. The page `media/chat.html` with `main.js` (32,016 lines) runs in a browser tab, as an installed PWA, in the `kiss-app` window and from an iOS Home Screen Web Clip (section 5).
- **Layers.** `src/kiss/server` (37 modules, 45,818 lines) is the daemon. `src/kiss/agents/sorcar` (50 modules, 64,809 lines) holds the agent, its tools and the synchronous client `daemon_client.py`, kept in that layer so that `run_agent` and the cron scheduler submit tasks without importing `kiss.server`. `src/kiss/core` (29 modules, 13,544 lines) and `src/kiss/core/models` (15 modules, 20,164 lines) hold the base agent and the model adapters.
- **Catalogue.** 84 commands at the previous inventory, 88 now. 21 left, among them the ten that the VS Code extension host consumed and the daemon discarded, the wake-word and frequent-task commands and the commit-message generator. 25 arrived: four for terminal tabs, four for machines, three for the sign-in window, three for Claude Code and Codex status and login, two for the browser tab, and `getTaskUsage`, `setChatSummary`, `deleteTask`, `deleteChat`, `openExternal`, `getReplayImage`, `cronAction`, `getRecommendations` and `dismissNotice`. `ServerApi.dispatch` validates every command against the catalogue and answers an invalid one with an `error` event; the page calls the catalogue through `media/api.js`.
- **Machines.** A tab bound to a registered machine has its commands forwarded to that machine's daemon and that daemon's frames relayed back (section 15).
- **Left the figure.** `WorktreeSorcarAgent` (folded into `SorcarAgent` 10-10), the spare-worktree pool and its prewarm (removed 10-09, section 12; the daemon still prewarms provider imports and the model registry) and the server's two voice-wake modules (the page detects the wake phrase, section 18).

## 5. Interfaces and prompt surfaces

| Surface | How it is reached | Notes |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Web app | `http://127.0.0.1:8787` on the daemon's machine, `https://<host>:8787` on the LAN, or the Cloudflare tunnel the daemon opens once a remote password is set; the LAN and tunnel addresses require the password, on the daemon's own machine too, and only a page opened at the loopback address (`127.0.0.1` or `localhost`) needs none | One page for every device, `chat.html` with `main.js` (32,016 lines) under `src/kiss/webapp/media/`; installable PWA with an offline shell (`sw.js`); light theme by default; reconnects in place with jittered exponential backoff and a 45 s half-open detector. Daemon side in section 14, page features in section 16 |
| Desktop window | `kiss-app`, with `--url`, `--wait`, `--foreground`, `--print-command`, `--install-launcher`, `--uninstall-launcher` and `--reveal-launcher` | The same page in a Chromium-family browser in app mode, with a launcher on Linux and macOS; added 10-16 (section 14) |
| iOS Home Screen | The "iPhone Home Screen" item of the page's "..." menu downloads `/webclip.mobileconfig` | A signed Web Clip profile that opens the page full screen; added 11-03 (section 14) |
| `sorcar` CLI **CHANGED** | `sorcar -t "task"` or `-f file` (exactly one), with `-m model`, `-b budget` and `--work-dir` (default `$KISS_WORKDIR`, then the current directory) | Runs the agent in the terminal process, without the daemon. Since 10-10 the agent is `ChatSorcarAgent` (`SorcarAgent` before), so the run is recorded in `history.db` with its result, usage and events, and it can create worktrees; any it leaves unmerged are named on stderr. `ask_user_question` is answered on the terminal. On a terminal it shows the live console view; with stdout piped it prints only the YAML result. Exit status 0 on success, 1 on failure, 2 on a usage error |
| Python client API **CHANGED** | `from kiss.server import sorcar; sorcar.run(task, ...)` returning `TaskResult(text, success, cost, tokens, steps, chat_id, task_id, settings, reconnects)` | Blocks until the task ends on the running daemon, found through `~/.kiss/sorcar-local.json` (`endpoint_file` or `KISS_SORCAR_LOCAL` name another file; a socket path before 10-01). 37 keyword options, 27 at the previous inventory: 9 left or were renamed (the worktree and auto-commit switches, the inline tool list and the socket path among them) and 19 arrived. Among the 37: `sea_path` (a file defining a `BaseSea` subclass whose `settings()` configures the run and whose `tools()` returns its toolset, loaded and run in the daemon), `sea_command`, `tool_profile`, `docker_image`, `machine` (the task runs on a registered machine, section 15), `attachments`, `budget_cap`, `compaction`, `cancel`, `timeout=3600`, `stop_on_timeout` and `reconnect_seconds=300` (a dropped connection is re-established and the task's events replayed; `reconnects` counts them). `settings` is the run's `task_settings` payload (section 6) |
| Headless SEA run | `headless.run_sea(sea, task, ...)`, or `scripts/run_sea.py <sea> "<task>"` from a checkout | One SEA in the calling process, run as a plain `SorcarAgent`, with no daemon and no page; its `run_agent` and `run_parallel` tools still need a running daemon; added 10-09 as a script and 10-31 as a module (section 9) |
| Slash commands | `/<name> <text>` at position 0 of a prompt | 72 commands in a stock checkout, registered from four sources; `/<name> help` and `/<name> check` answer without running the SEA (section 9) |
| Channels | `kiss-<channel>` CLIs, `run_agent(agent="slack", ...)`, always-on gateways scheduled by cron | 44 agents (section 19) |
| Scheduler | `kiss-cron`, the `/cron` command, `run_agent(agent="cron", ...)` and the Schedule panel | Section 20 |
| Voice | In-page wake phrase "Hey Sorcar"; `talk()` playback in every open page and, for a task a same-machine API client started, on the daemon machine's speakers | Section 18 |

The VS Code extension, a row of this table in the previous inventory, was removed 10-14. A client that authenticates with the local token and sends neither a `User-Agent` nor an `Origin` header is taken for an old copy of the extension and is answered with a notice naming the web app's address; when `install.sh` finds and uninstalls a copy, every page shows a toast about it for 7 days, or until its "Do not show this" box is ticked (`dismissNotice`). The root `sorcar` script of the repository still launches `kiss.agents.sorcar.worktree_sorcar_agent`, a module deleted 10-10, and so fails; the `sorcar` console script is the working entry point. `pyproject.toml` declares 53 console scripts, 50 at the previous inventory: the 44 `kiss-<channel>` CLIs, `sorcar`, `kiss-web`, `kiss-app`, `kiss-cron`, `sea` (`sea lint` and `sea docs`, section 9), `check`, `impacted-tests`, `generate-api-docs` and `swedefend-eval`.

Prompt assets shipped in `src/kiss/`:

- `SYSTEM.md` (6,392 words) **CHANGED**, 3,971 words at the previous inventory: identity and rule precedence, visibility constraint, tool usage rules, context and continuation, periodic summaries, voice, web research protocol (10 sites, 1 for real-time data), code style, pre-flight checks, a six-step "How to Work" procedure (added 09-30), adversarial testing and training, deep work, complex task planning, file browsing, desktop apps, testing, pre-finish verification, Sorcar-specific rules. The 7-step AI discovery loop left the file 10-27 and now ships with the `/discover` SEA (section 3). With prompt caching on (the default), the Anthropic adapter sends the static part under its own `cache_control` breakpoint, so runs with the same tools and static part read one cache entry; the per-task tail (work directory, process id, the "Task Settings" block, `AGENTS.md`) follows as a block with no breakpoint of its own and is cached with the run's conversation instead.
- `SYSTEM_LITE.md` (1,074 words): the reduced prompt for a task the classifier marks as simple and for a run that cannot edit files (sections 6 and 11); `/ask` ships its own `_ask_system_lite.md` (515 words) plus an answering playbook.
- Both prompts and `TIPS.md` carry brand placeholders that `render_brand` fills from `brand.json` when the file is read: the identity sentence and the state directory in the two prompts, the product name and the state directory in `TIPS.md`. `SYSTEM.md` also carries a version placeholder, filled with the package version of `src/kiss/core/_version.py`, the same for every brand. A white-label build therefore changes no prompt file (section 14). The repository and website addresses moved from `SYSTEM.md` into the brand's `identity` on 10-18.
- `INJECTIONS.md` **CHANGED**: 2 promptlets since 10-17, 6 at the previous inventory. They are listed after those of `~/.kiss/MY_INJECTION.md`, which is created with one starter promptlet and is editable in place from the Inject panel.
- `TIPS.md`: 22 tips (23 at the previous inventory), shown once per version in each browser profile until the reader opts out, which creates `~/.kiss/TIPS_DISABLED`.
- `SAMPLE_TASKS.md` (894 words, 12 sample tasks) is still shipped, but no code has read it since the welcome page's sample-task chips were removed on 09-26.

Files a user keeps in `~/.kiss/` (`$KISS_HOME` when set; a white-label brand names another directory with `home_dir`) that shape a run:

- `AGENTS.md`: standing instructions appended to every system prompt except that of a `plain_io` run, managed by `/remember` and `/forget`. The file was `SORCAR.md` until 10-03, and a file left under the old name is adopted on first use (section 6).
- `SEAS.md`: extra SEA folders, one per line, a later line beating an earlier one (section 9).
- `MY_INJECTION.md`: the user's promptlets, above.
- `MY_MODELS.json`: model entries that override or extend the bundled catalogue; top-level keys starting with `_` are comments (section 8).
- `mcp.json` and `skills/`: MCP servers and skills, read together with their project-level and cross-client counterparts (section 7).
- `config.json` and `api_keys.env`: settings and credentials, written by the Settings panel (sections 6 and 16).
- The personal task-template file (MY_TASK_TEMPLATES.md), auto-created there until 09-26, is read by nothing.

## 6. Agent runtime

`KISSAgent` runs one model conversation, `RelentlessAgent` chains such sessions until the task is done, `SorcarAgent` adds the tools, the system prompt, the guards and the finish gate, and `ChatSorcarAgent` adds chat persistence. Tools are in section 7, models in section 8, SEA hooks in section 9, sub-agent dispatch in section 10 and worktrees in section 12.

### KISSAgent (`src/kiss/core/kiss_agent.py`, 3,017 lines)

- **Loop**: `run(model_name, prompt_template, arguments, system_prompt, tools, is_agentic, max_steps, max_budget, model_config, printer, verbose, attachments, print_prompts, llm_call_hook, tool_call_hook, llm_response_hook, compaction)`, with defaults of 10,000 steps and $10. A step compacts the conversation when due, calls the model and runs the returned tool calls in order (calls after `finish` in the same turn do not run). Each step's tool result ends with a usage footer: steps, context tokens, the step's tool-output tokens, the model call's cost, its output tokens and stop reason, total tokens and budget spent against the cap.
- **Ending**: `finish`; the step or budget bound; the context limit (`KISS_CONTEXT_LIMIT_FRACTION`, 0.7 of the model's window), which hands the task to the next RelentlessAgent session; 3 consecutive errors; 12 consecutive stalled streams, counted separately **NEW**. A second consecutive text-only turn ends the run as an implicit `finish` with `success: true`; two empty replies raise an error instead.
- **Recovery** **NEW**: a reply cut by the output-token cap is retried once at effort `low` (11-01), unless the model's catalogue entry has no `thinking` level or the request already ran at `low`, `minimal` or `none`. A refusal, a content-filter stop, a cap cut-off that gets no such retry or repeated empty replies switch the run to a model of another family (11-01) or, when no other family is configured, to the `get_fallback_model()` answer of the model's own family; when no switch is possible, a refusal is retried once with a message restating the task's context (10-26); a provider's content-policy HTTP error counts as a content-filter stop. Non-retryable errors (authentication, permission, missing or invalid API key, `not_found_error`, "credit balance is too low") switch to `get_fallback_model()`: the catalogue's declared fallback, else the model's OpenRouter twin when `OPENROUTER_API_KEY` is set. A run switches model this way once, and the switch is printed as `Switched model from X to Y after <reason>` (section 8).
- **Hooks**: `llm_call_hook(new_messages)` before each model call, `tool_call_hook(name, args)` before each tool call and `llm_response_hook(reply)` after each reply **NEW** (10-31), which receives an `LLMReply` (`llm_reply.py`: `text`, `tool_calls`, `usage`, `step`, `budget_used`, `total_tokens_used`). The layers above also install `pre_step_hook`, `tool_call_guard`, `tool_result_hook`, `helper_charge_hook`, `budget_check_hook`, `budget_status_hook` and `context_reset_hook`. What a hook may answer is under "Finish gates and guards"; the SEA methods of the same names are in section 9.
- **Tool-output compaction** **CHANGED** (`context_compaction.py`, 686 lines): before a model call, once the context has passed 100K tokens and then every further 100K, or at any step once it has passed the target, the larger of 100K tokens and 30% of the model's window (11-01; 100K when the window is unknown), and never sooner than 10 steps after the previous compaction (10-25), old tool results and tool-call arguments are replaced by stubs.
  - Results longer than 500 characters and older than the six most recent are stubbed with a 200-character preview, never those of `Edit`, `Write` or `finish`. Arguments are stubbed with a 120-character preview (11-01), never those of `finish`; the stub of an `Edit` or `Write` argument points at the file.
  - A compaction is applied only when it pays for the prompt-cache miss it causes: it must drop 25% of the context below the target and 10% above it, each raised to a payback floor derived from the model's cache-read price (28% at Anthropic's 5% cache reads, 15% at OpenAI's 10%). None runs once the context has reached 80% of the hand-off limit.
  - Each stub carries an id under which the run keeps the original: a call whose argument is a whole stub runs on the original text, and a partial or unknown stub is refused.
  - DeepSeek-family models, whose API wants every earlier turn's reasoning passed back, lose whole turns instead: all but the newest 10 (11-08).
  - Every compaction is logged as a `compaction` event (`step`, `context_tokens`, `dropped_tokens`, `results`, `arguments`, `target_tokens`; a stub compaction adds `reasoning_blocks`, a whole-turn removal `turns`). `compaction=False` on `run()`, the SEA setting or the global `compaction` setting turns it off (11-05).
- **Bulk clearing of a Claude model's old tool calls** **NEW** (11-11, replacing the server-side clearing of 11-08): a Claude model on the Anthropic API (not Bedrock, which keeps the compaction thresholds above) has its old tool calls and its old reasoning cleared by the agent, in the stored conversation: past 250,000 tokens of context the inputs and results of all but the last 20 tool calls are stubbed and the old thinking blocks removed in one edit, and only when that removes at least 50,000 tokens, so the cached prefix is re-written only then. **CHANGED** (11-11): the two numbers hold for a model whose cache write costs fifty cache reads (Claude Fable 5.2 and 5.1) and are multiplied by the square root of another model's ratio over fifty (177,000 and 35,000 for Opus and Sonnet 5.5 at 25; 125,000 and 25,000 for the other Claude models at 12.5), the trigger is at most 80% of the hand-off limit (112,000 on a 200,000-token window, which was never cleared before), and the minimum counts the reasoning that goes (a reply's output tokens less its visible text). Replayed on the 19,174 Claude Fable 5.2 calls of 11-11, this clears once per 78 calls, 176,000 tokens at a time, and 3 sessions reach the hand-off limit instead of 23 under 200,000 and 100,000 with the reasoning left out of the count. Anthropic's server-side `clear_tool_uses` edit is no longer requested: its boundary moved by a result or two between requests, and each move re-wrote the prompt cache without freeing anything (312 times on 11-11). Claude 5.1 and later, which drop a thinking block sent back after an earlier message changed or (Fable 5.2) refuse the request, lose every thinking block at a clearing; an earlier model keeps those of its newest reply. `KISS_REASONING_KEEP_TURNS` (0, the default, asks for nothing) has two provider-specific forms: on the Anthropic API the value is the number of turns whose thinking the server keeps, sent as a further edit on requests with thinking on, and the Responses API adapter sends an OpenAI model from GPT-5.6 on `reasoning.context: current_turn` for any positive value, unless the run's `model_config` sets `reasoning.context` itself; other adapters ignore the setting. While a model's requests carry either form, the agent starts a new turn every 5 steps (`KISS_REASONING_TURN_STEPS`) with a short user note. With `KISS_CONTEXT_EDIT_TRIGGER_TOKENS=0` the agent compacts such a model by the thresholds above; the compaction switches above turn the bulk clearing off too, and not the reasoning edit. A clearing is a `compaction` event (`reasoning_blocks` counts the thinking blocks removed); what the server cleared under the reasoning edit is a `context_edits` event.
- **Routine turns** **NEW** (10-25): the turn after a refused call, a `summary` or memory tool (`memory_delete` excepted), a `bash_job` or `agent_job` wait that ended with the job still running, or an unchanged `Read` on the host is flagged `routine` in its `llm_call` event; when a turn makes several calls, the last one other than `finish` decides. `KISS_ROUTINE_TURN_EFFORT` (empty by default) runs such turns at a lower reasoning effort on models whose catalogue entry has a `thinking` level.
- **Prompt-cache keep-alive** **CHANGED** (`prompt_cache_keepalive.py`, 233 lines): fan-out tools (`run_parallel`, `run_commands_parallel`, `run_agent`), `ask_user_question`, `bash_job` and `agent_job` waits and any tool call with a timeout of 300 s or more run under a thread that pings the model's prompt cache every 240 s. The number of pings now follows the model's prices (10-25): `int(0.8 × (cache-write − cache-read) / cache-read)`, at least 1 and at most 60 (4 hours), or 10 when the prices are unknown. Each ping is billed and recorded as an `llm_call` event.
- **Native tool calling** **NEW** (10-17): `codex/*` models receive the tools as an MCP server over bridge threads; `finish` and tools that must run between turns (`set_model`) stay on the text protocol (section 8).
- **Per-call cost events**: every billed model call emits `llm_call` with `model`, `step`, `duration_ms`, `input_tokens`, `output_tokens`, `cache_read`, `cache_write`, `cost` and three newer fields **NEW**: `routine` and `effort` (10-25) and `stop_reason` (10-28). Up to nine token counts are priced (input, output, cache read, 5-minute and 1-hour cache writes, audio in and out, audio cache read, image output); a cost reported by the provider wins over the catalogue price; a failed call that was billed is charged from its partial usage; the spend of a vision-bridge helper is filed under the helper's own model (11-06).
- **Stop**: `stop_signal.py` (58 lines) publishes each task thread's stop event, and a stop request sends no further model request. `tool_interrupt.py` (612 lines) stops one tool call from its panel: a cooperative event first, a forced exception in the tool's thread after 1.0 s, and the result `User interrupted the tool call.`; nested calls are registered under their parent.
- **Tool errors**: an exception in a tool returns `Failed to call <name> with <args>: <error>` followed by the expected signature and, for an unknown argument, a hint.

### RelentlessAgent (`src/kiss/agents/sorcar/relentless_agent.py`, 2,065 lines)

- Runs a fresh `KISSAgent` per session (`<name> Session-N`) until the task finishes, for at most `max_sub_sessions` (10,000) sessions. `run()` takes 18 parameters: KISSAgent's without `is_agentic` and `print_prompts`, plus `work_dir`, `max_sub_sessions` and `docker_image`. Defaults: model `claude-opus-4-6`, budget $200; the app passes its `max_budget` setting instead, default $100.
- **Hand-off** **CHANGED** (10-25): a session of more than one step that ends on the context limit, or on a `KISSError` with no underlying exception, is summarised from a scripted digest (`handoff_digest.py`, 257 lines) of the task text (3,000 characters), the last 3 `summary` texts (1,500 characters each), up to 40 written files with the top-level symbols of the Python ones, the last 5 failing tool calls (600 characters of each result) and the last 40 tool calls (300 characters of each result). Any other error, or one in a session's first step, ends the run with a failed result. A summariser with no tools but `finish` writes the narrative from it (3 steps at most); it replaced a summariser that read files and ran commands. The next session starts with `# Task Progress (Continuation N)` over the summaries of the sessions so far, the oldest dropped first to fit 60,000 characters. The run stops after 2 zero-progress sessions: no tool call other than `finish`, or a summary identical to the previous one.
- **Budget exhaustion**: a sub-agent that runs out of budget returns a partial result that quotes up to 8 of its steps (600 characters each) after the summaries of its earlier sessions; a top-level run raises.
- **Framework instructions**: every session's system prompt gets `IMPORTANT_INSTRUCTIONS`, a Task Settings block (model name, maximum budget, starting time, user id, IP address, OS, machine info) and `~/.kiss/AGENTS.md`, renamed from `SORCAR.md` on 10-03 (a legacy file is adopted) and read with writer-tolerant IO so a rename in progress cannot abort the task. A plain-I/O run gets none of them.
- **Usage ledger**: an append-only ledger with epochs and de-duplication by source and sequence number, so sub-agent, classifier, TTS and side-channel spend are counted exactly once, including across stop interrupts; printer offsets never decrease, so the UI cost never drops. The step footer shows the task's cumulative spend against its cap **NEW** (10-20).
- **Docker and remote execution**: `docker_image=` starts a container through `DockerManager` (`docker_manager.py`, 1,663 lines) with the task work dir bind-mounted at its host path and set as `WorkingDir`; `container:<name-or-id>` attaches to a running container without mounts and leaves it running; `exec:<url>` runs the tools on a remote environment through an exec bridge (`exec_bridge.py`, 488 lines) **NEW** (10-26). Container output streams as `bash_stream` events. Every exec is tagged, so a timed-out or stopped command is killed inside the container together with the processes it spawned. The shell and edit guards below apply in the container as on the host (section 21).

### SorcarAgent (`sorcar_agent.py`, 5,132 lines)

- `run()` takes 32 parameters: RelentlessAgent's plus `web_tools`, `is_parallel`, `current_editor_file`, `ask_user_question_callback`, `base_system_prompt`, `append_basic_tools`, `inherited_tools`, `use_memory`, `tool_profile`, `live_browser`, `prompt_suffix`, `system_prompt_hook`, `tools_hook` and `task_begun`.
- Resolved once per run:
  - the model: the caller's, else the last one picked, else the default (section 8);
  - the tool profile: the explicit one, else `review` for a reviewer sub-agent whose task is not an implementation task (`KISS_TOOL_PROFILES`), else `full` (section 7);
  - the system prompt: `SYSTEM.md`, or the lite prompt when the classifier calls the task simple or the run cannot edit files (section 11). `base_system_prompt` replaces either, and a SEA's `system_prompt()` returns the final text (section 9). A restricted-profile note lists only the tools the run really has, `WEB_TOOLS_OFF_NOTE` is added when browsing is off, and the memory protocol with a recall for the task text is appended (section 13). Task Settings gain the parallel mode and the scratch dir (`./tmp/`, or `./tmp/<task-id>/` under a chat).
- **Tool set**: the built-ins of the profile plus the caller's tools; `append_basic_tools=False` leaves only `finish` and the caller's tools; a SEA's `tools()` sees that set without `finish`, which is added after the hook returns and cannot be removed by it, and of two tools of one name in the list it returns the later wins (sections 7 and 9).
- `set_model` switches the model between turns: the conversation is handed over and the tool schema rebuilt for the new model. `summary` is a no-op tool; its every-10-steps cadence is no longer left to the prompt alone (see the summary cadence guard below).
- **Follow-up messages**: a message the user sends while the task runs is injected before the next step as `User says: <message>. Take the message into account and finish your task.`, with its attachments in one more user message (attachments since 10-20).
- **Background jobs** stay on the agent, so a follow-up prompt in the same chat can still wait for, tail or stop them (section 7).
- **Sub-agents**: a child's default budget share is `remaining / (num_tasks + 1)`; the review fan-out quotas removed on 09-25 have not returned (section 10).
- **Worktree tools**: `create_worktree` and `commit_and_merge` live on `SorcarAgent` itself; `WorktreeSorcarAgent` was removed on 10-10 **CHANGED** and the `is_worktree` setting on 10-09 (section 12).
- **End of run**: the browser is closed, the remaining `run_agent` jobs are stopped, the marker of an unmerged worktree is kept, classifier spend is folded in, the run hooks are released and the usage totals emitted, each step protected from the failure of the one before. An unexpected exception becomes a `success: false` result; `KISSError` and `SeaError` propagate. Warnings collected during the run are emitted as `warning` events.
- **Command line**: the `sorcar` console script runs a `ChatSorcarAgent` on `-t TASK` or `-f FILE`, with `-m/--model`, `-b/--max-budget` and `--work-dir` (default `$KISS_WORKDIR`, else the current directory); it exits 0 when the result reports success and 1 otherwise (section 5).

### ChatSorcarAgent (`chat_sorcar_agent.py`, 1,315 lines)

- Persists chats and task chains in `~/.kiss/history.db`: it allocates the chat id and the `task_history` row, records the budget, the SEA name of the run (`task_history.sea`) and the cron flag, broadcasts `new_tab`, `tasks_updated` and `task_settings`, and saves the result. `run()` takes `prompt_template` first and 36 parameters in all, 5 of them private to the daemon.
